# 12 Developmental and metabolic plasticity of white-skinned grape berries in 13 response to *Botrytis cinerea* during noble rot

## 27 Summary

- 28 Noble rot causes major reprogramming of grape berry metabolism by activating stress responses
- and ripening processes, including pathways that are inactive or with limited flux in white-skinned
- 30 berries.

31

### 38 **ABSTRACT**

39 Noble rot results from exceptional infections of ripe grape (*Vitis vinifera*) berries by *Botrytis*  40 *cinerea*. Unlike bunch rot, noble rot promotes favorable changes in grape berries and 41 accumulation of secondary metabolites that enhance wine grape composition. Noble rot-infected 42 berries of Sémillon, a white-skinned variety, were collected over three years from a commercial 43 vineyard at the same time fruit were harvested for botrytized wine production. Using an 44 integrated transcriptomics and metabolomics approach, we demonstrate that noble rot alters the 45 metabolism of Sémillon berries by inducing biotic and abiotic stress responses as well as ripening 46 processes. During noble rot, *Botrytis* induced the expression of key regulators of ripening-47 associated pathways, some of which are distinctive to normal ripening of red-skinned cultivars. 48 Enhancement of phenylpropanoid metabolism, characterized by a restricted flux in white-skinned 49 berries, was a common outcome of noble rot and red-skinned berry ripening. Transcript and 50 metabolite analyses together with enzymatic assays determined that the biosynthesis of 51 anthocyanins is a consistent hallmark of noble rot in Sémillon berries. The biosynthesis of 52 terpenes and fatty acid aroma precursors also increased during noble rot. We finally characterized 53 the impact of noble rot in botrytized wines. Altogether the results of this work demonstrate that 54 noble rot causes a major reprogramming of berry development and metabolism. This desirable 55 interaction between a fruit and a fungus stimulates pathways otherwise inactive in white-skinned 56 berries, leading to greater accumulation of compounds involved in the unique flavor and aroma of 57 botrytized wines.

### 58 **INTRODUCTION**

59 Noble rot results from atypical *Botrytis cinerea* (*Botrytis*, henceforth) infections of ripe or 60 overripe grape berries that occur under particular environmental conditions (Magyar, 2011; 61 Vannini and Chilosi, 2013). White-skinned grape berries affected by noble rot are used to 62 produce some of the highest quality dessert wines in the world, known as botrytized wines. 63 Botrytized wines have been made since the XVI century, primarily in the Tokaj (Hungary), 64 Rheingau (Germany) and Sauternais (France) regions. Their production has extended more 65 recently to Italy, Australia, New Zealand, South Africa, and California (Magyar, 2011). These 66 naturally sweet wines have distinctive flavor and aroma profiles, which include pineapple, apricot, 67 pear, raisin, clove, and honey features, combined with *roti* (i.e., roasted) characters (Magyar, 68 2011; Louis Teissedre and Donèche, 2013).

69 *Botrytis* is a necrotrophic fungal pathogen with a broad host range (Choquer et al., 2007). Under 70 specific climatic (i.e., moist nights, foggy mornings and dry days) and edaphic (i.e., low nutrient 71 and well-drained soils) conditions, *Botrytis* infections may occur slowly causing noble rot 72 (Ribéreau-Gayon et al., 1980). This preferred type of infection promotes the accumulation of 73 aroma and flavor compounds as well as the concentration of sugars (Vannini and Chilosi, 2013). 74 In contrast, heavy rainfalls and high humidity conditions favor a more aggressive type of *Botrytis* 75 colonization of grape berries known as bunch rot (Gubler et al., 2013). As opposed to noble rot, 76 bunch rot negatively impacts wine production by interfering with the fermentation process and 77 altering the sensory properties of the resulting product (Hornsey, 2007; Morales-Valle et al., 78 2011). The molecular and biochemical processes that lead to either noble rot or bunch rot during 79 *Botrytis*-grape berry interactions have not been elucidated yet. It has been proposed that the 80 combinatory effects of the environment, cultural practices, intrinsic characteristics of the grape 81 cultivar, and the berry microbiome influence the type of rot developed (Ribéreau-Gayon et al., 82 1980; Sipiczki, 2006).

83 Noble rot causes two distinctive phenotypic alterations in the white-skinned berries that do not 84 occur during bunch rot: (i) color change of the skin to pink and subsequently to purple-brown, 85 and (ii) dehydration at the final stages of infection caused by cracks in the cuticle layers. The 86 latter explains the high sugar concentration in fully rotted and shriveled (i.e., *pourri roti*) berries 87 (Magyar, 2006). Physicochemical changes induced by noble rot in grape berries include higher 88 levels of sugar alcohols (i.e., glycerol, arabitol, mannitol, sorbitol and inositol), enhanced sugar 89 content, reduced glucose to fructose ratio, higher concentration of malic acid and lower 90 concentration of tartaric acid, when compared to uninfected berries (Ribéreau-Gayon et al., 2006).

5

91 Mucic acid and gluconic acid, two organic acids that result from *Botrytis* catabolism of plant 92 sugars, also accumulate in noble-rotted berries. Infected berries usually have high concentrations 93 of free galacturonic acid, rhamnose, galactose, mannose, arabinose and xylose, which may result 94 from the *Botrytis*-induced breakdown of pectin and cross-linking glycans present in the fruit cell 95 walls (Magyar, 2011). Greater abundance of total polyphenolic compounds has also been detected 96 in berries affected by noble rot (Magyar, 2006). However, the effects of noble rot in the chemical 97 composition of the berries vary depending on the grape cultivar, *terroir,* and degree of *Botrytis* 98 infection. To date, only targeted compositional analyses have been performed in noble-rotted 99 berries. Comprehensive metabolomic approaches will help to further understand the influence of 100 noble rot in grape berry metabolism.

101 Due to their high predisposition to noble rot, grapevine cultivars commonly used for botrytized 102 wines include Sémillon, Riesling, Sauvignon blanc, Muscadel and Chenin blanc (Magyar, 2011). 103 The compact cluster architecture and berry cuticle with lower mechanical resistance of these 104 white-skinned varieties seem to facilitate *Botrytis* penetration (Vail and Marois, 1991; 105 Kretschmer et al., 2007). During ripening, grape berries undergo a series of complex biochemical 106 and physiological processes, which enhance their quality for wine production but also their 107 vulnerability to pathogen infection (Pezet et al., 2003; Deytieux-Belleau et al., 2009). Some 108 ripening processes that may promote *Botrytis* infections of grape berries are cell wall and cuticle 109 modifications that lead to fruit softening and micro-fractures on the berry surface, modulation of 110 the fruit´s synthesis and perception of plant hormones, changes in the levels of organic acids and 111 sugars, and the loss of preformed defenses (Miedes and Lorences, 2007; Cantu et al., 2008; Cantu 112 et al., 2009; Centeno et al., 2011; Prusky et al., 2013; Blanco-Ulate et al., 2013a). Moreover, 113 *Botrytis* infections can accelerate some of these ripening processes in unripe fruit (Cantu et al., 114 2009; Blanco-Ulate et al., 2013a; Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015).

115 In this study, we integrated transcriptomics, metabolomics, and enzyme activity assays to 116 characterize the impact of noble rot on the development and metabolism of white-skinned grape 117 berries under field conditions. Berries at distinct stages of noble rot were sampled during multiple 118 years from a commercial vineyard. We identified grape molecular pathways that are triggered 119 during distinct stages of noble rot. Some of these pathways correspond to plant responses to biotic 120 and abiotic stresses, while others are involved in the generation of aroma and flavor metabolites. 121 Particular metabolic changes induced by noble rot were also similar to events that occur during 122 normal development of red-skinned berries, including the induction of pathways of secondary 123 metabolism that have limited flux in white-skinned cultivars. We detected in noble-rotted berries 124 enhanced expression of transcription factors that are known to regulate secondary metabolic 125 pathways during berry ripening. We validated the relevance of these pathways during noble rot 126 using targeted gene expression and enzymatic analyses in *Botrytis*-infected berries harvested 127 during two additional years. Finally, we profiled the metabolites of commercial botrytized wines 128 produced from the same vineyard where the berries were collected, and corroborated that key 129 compounds that result from noble rot are carried over to the wines.

130

131

#### 132 **RESULTS**

#### 133 **Sampling of naturally occurring** *Botrytis* **infections**

134 Ripe grape berries (*Vitis vinifera* cv. Sémillon; soluble solids 23.9 ± 1.0 %m/m) displaying noble 135 rot symptoms were harvested in 2012, 2013 and 2014 from a commercial vineyard in Napa 136 (California). Samples were collected on the same day as grapes were harvested for botrytized 137 wine production. *Botrytis* infections occurred naturally in the field and were categorized in three 138 stages of noble rot development. The initial stage of infection (stage 1, Figure 1) was 139 characterized by a color change of the berries from yellow to pink. As the infection progressed, 140 the berry skin became softer and dark pink (stage 2, Figure 1). Berries at stage 3 (i.e., *pourry*  141 *plein*) were fully rotten, but not dry. Stage 3 berries presented cracked skins with evident mycelia 142 growth and sporulation, and were purple-brown (Figure 1). Berries that did not show any 143 symptoms of infection were harvested simultaneously and used as control (Figure 1). 144 Measurements of *Botrytis* biomass using the monoclonal antibody BC12.CA4 (Meyer and Dewey, 145 2000) in the collected samples confirmed that the stages correspond to distinct degrees of fungal 146 infection, and presented very similar patterns during the three independent harvesting seasons 147 (Figure 1). Two-way factorial ANOVA demonstrated a significant effect of infection stage on *Botrytis* biomass (*P* < 2.20×10-16 148 ), while the year of harvest (*P =* 0.23) and the infection stage by 149 year interaction (*P =* 0.59) were not significant.

150 Sugars, sugar alcohols, and organic acids were quantified as previously reported (Magyar, 2011; 151 Louis Teissedre and Donèche, 2013). As expected, glycerol, mannitol and sorbitol levels 152 increased significantly (*P* ≤ 0.01) in the infected samples (Supplemental Table S1). Although 153 sucrose and fructose accumulated as a result of noble rot, only a slight reduction in the 154 glucose/fructose ratio was observed at stages 2 and 3 of infection (Supplemental Table S1).

155

## 156 **Integration of transcriptomics and metabolomics to study** *Botrytis* **infections of grape**

157 **berries**

158 RNAseq and metabolomic analyses were performed with samples harvested in 2012. mRNA was 159 isolated from four biological replicates per infection stage (i.e., stages 1, 2 and 3) and control, and 160 sequenced using an Illumina HiSeq2000 platform. Supplemental Table S2 provides a summary of 161 the parsed reads from all samples as well as the number of reads that mapped onto the predicted 162 transcriptomes of *Botrytis* (strains B05.10 and BcDW1) and *V. vinifera* (cv. PN40024). The 163 expression of 15,082 *Botrytis* genes (92.54% of total *Botrytis* genes) and 24,373 grape genes 164 (81.32% of total grape genes) was detected across all stages of noble rot and control samples. The

- 165 relative abundance of grape to fungal transcripts decreased at later stages of infection reflecting
- 166 the increase in fungal biomass with the progression of noble rot; however most grape genes
- 167 (71.34% of total genes) were still detectable at advanced stages of infection (Supplemental Table
- 168 S2). *Botrytis* genes were functionally annotated based on similarity to ascomycete peptides in
- 169 GenBank and gene ontology terms were assigned using Blast2GO (Conesa et al., 2005). The
- 170 VitisNet functional annotations were used to assign grape genes to particular functional
- 171 categories and sub-categories (Grimplet et al., 2009).

172 Detection and quantification of metabolites in noble-rotted (stages 1, 2 and 3) and control berries 173 were carried out by UHPLC/QTOF-MS using five biological replicates and six technical 174 replications. A total of 2,163 non-redundant mass features were identified among all samples, 175 comprising products of both plant and fungal metabolism (Supplemental Table S3). The possible 176 identities of the mass features were determined by comparisons to the METLIN database (< 30 177 ppm cut-off; (Smith et al., 2005)). Based on statistical testing (i.e., multiple factor and cluster 178 analyses), a set of 688 metabolites with significant accumulation patterns were manually curated 179 by evaluating their retention times and by matching their MS/MS spectra to the Tandem Mass 180 Spectrum Database (see Materials and Methods for details). The curated metabolites were then 181 classified into compound classes and subclasses using the chemical taxonomy of the Human 182 Metabolome Database (Wishart et al., 2012).

183 RNAseq and metabolomics data were integrated into a multiple factor analysis (MFA) to 184 determine if the distinct stages of noble rot could be discerned based on the abundance of (i) 185 *Botrytis* transcripts, (ii) grape transcripts, and (iii) mass features. Figure 1 displays the results of 186 the MFA, which included measurements of *Botrytis* biomass as an additional class of quantitative 187 variable. The MFA revealed that the noble rot stages were significantly different (95% 188 confidence) from each other at both transcriptome and metabolome levels. The first dimension 189 (DIM1, 29.82%) distinguished the heavily infected samples (i.e., stages 2 and 3) from samples at 190 stage 1 and the asymptomatic controls. The second dimension (DIM2, 9.72%) further separated 191 the samples belonging to stage 1 and control (Figure 1). Eigenvalues and percentage of explained 192 variance for the 15 dimensions of the MFA are provided in Supplemental Table S4.

193 Quantitative variables (i.e., gene transcripts and mass feature abundances) that significantly 194 correlated (-0.70 ≥ *R* ≥ 0.70, *P* ≤ 0.01) with the first two dimensions of the MFA were analyzed 195 to provide insights into the molecular pathways responsible for the development of noble rot 196 (Supplemental Table S5). Enrichment analyses (*P* ≤ 0.05) of gene ontology (GO) terms using 197 *Botrytis* genes with significant correlations identified functions employed by the pathogen during 198 berry infection (Supplemental Figure S1). At initial stages of noble rot (i.e., positive DIM2), 199 *Botrytis* expressed functions related with cell growth and host tissue colonization. These included 200 the expression of genes involved in plant cell wall degradation (e.g., endo-β-1,4-glucanases; 201 (Blanco-Ulate et al., 2014)) and the metabolism of reactive oxygen species (e.g. superoxide 202 dismutase; Supplemental Table S5). Another function that was induced early during *Botrytis* 203 infections was the biosynthesis of unsaturated fatty acids. At stages 2 and 3 of noble rot (i.e., 204 positive DIM1), the transport of organic molecules (e.g., carbohydrates) was a prominent feature. 205 This is in agreement with the necrotrophic lifestyle of *Botrytis* once established in the berry 206 tissues. Potential virulence factors were identified among the *Botrytis* genes with the highest significant correlations (*R* ≥ 0.90; *P* ≤ 1.0×10-6 207 ) to the positive sides of DIM1 and DIM2 208 (Supplemental Table S5; (Amselem et al., 2011; Giesbert et al., 2012; Yang et al., 2013; Ambrose 209 et al., 2015). These comprised two putative salicylate hydroxylases (*BC1T\_14568* and 210 *BC1T\_15387*), a protein-tyrosine phosphatase (PTP*; BC1T\_07726*), a serine/threonine protein 211 phosphatase type 2A (PP2A; *BC1T\_05741*), and a non-ribosomal protein synthase (NRPS-like, 212 *BC1T\_01317*).

213 Grape genes whose transcript accumulation significantly correlated to each of the main MFA 214 dimensions (Supplemental Table S5) were evaluated to determine plant cellular and metabolic 215 processes altered in response to noble rot (Supplemental Figure S1). Enriched biological 216 functions (*P* ≤ 0.01) associated with stages 1 and 2 (i.e., positive DIM2) included: terpene 217 metabolism, anthocyanin biosynthesis, and fatty acid metabolism. Stages 2 and 3 (i.e., positive 218 DIM1) presented significant enrichments in a variety of functions, such as shikimate metabolism, 219 phenylpropanoid metabolism, alkaloid metabolism, argine and proline metabolism, 220 monosaccharide and polysaccharide metabolism, ethylene and jasmonic acid (JA) signaling, and 221 responses to abiotic stress (e.g., oxidative stress and wounding).

222 Compound classes associated with noble rot were identified using metabolite variables with 223 significant correlations to the major dimensions (Supplemental Table S5). The abundance of only 224 18 metabolites correlated significantly with stages 1 and 2 (i.e., positive DIM2), most of which 225 corresponded to compound classes with relevant functions in responses to plant stress as well as 226 in grape aroma and flavor composition, such as benzene derivatives, cinnamic acid derivatives 227 and flavonoids (Supplemental Figure S1). The 133 metabolites that showed greater accumulation 228 in heavily noble-rotted samples (i.e. positive DIM1) included fatty acids, prenol lipids, benzene 229 derivatives, cinnamic acid derivatives and peptides (Supplemental Figure S1).

230 The MFA results provided an initial survey of relevant fungal and plant functions that occur 231 throughout the progression of noble rot. Due to the distinctive and prominent effects of noble rot 232 on grape berry metabolism, we focused our subsequent analyses on characterizing changes in 233 grape transcript levels and metabolite abundances at specific stages of infection.

234

## 235 **Grape berry responses to noble rot**

Grape genes that have significant differential expression (DE genes, *Padj* 236 ≤ 0.05) at distinct stages 237 of noble rot were determined by comparisons between samples from each stage and the 238 asymptomatic control using DESeq2 (Love et al., 2014). The DE genes were grouped in 23 239 clusters according to their patterns of expression across all comparisons (Supplemental Table S6). 240 Among these, 7 clusters included only up-regulated genes in response to noble rot (Figure 2). 241 Metabolite data were processed following a similar strategy. To detect compounds with 242 significant changes in abundance, statistical tests between samples at each infection stage and the 243 asymptomatic controls were performed for each metabolite. Metabolites with statistically significant changes (*Padj* 244 ≤ 0.05) were grouped in 16 distinct clusters based on their patterns of 245 accumulation across the stages of noble rot (Supplemental Table S7). Six clusters consisted of 246 metabolites with increased abundance due to *Botrytis* infections (Figure 3).

247 Biological processes activated as a result of noble rot were determined by enrichment analyses (*P* 248 ≤ 0.01) of functional sub-categories in the clusters of up-regulated genes (Figure 2 and 249 Supplemental Table S8) and by identifying compound classes in the clusters with metabolites that 250 significantly accumulated in infected samples (Figure 3). Distinct families of transcription factors 251 were induced as a function of *Botrytis* infection (Supplemental Table S8). Among these, 252 APETALA2/Ethylene Responsive Factors (AP2-ERF), which play important roles in plant-253 pathogen interactions and fruit ripening (Gutterson and Reuber, 2004; Licausi et al., 2013), were 254 up-regulated at all stages of noble rot (gene cluster 01). The expression of NAM/ATAF/CUC 255 (NAC) transcription factors, which regulate key plant developmental processes such as fruit 256 ripening and senescence (Kou et al., 2014; Podzimska-Sroka et al., 2015), increased throughout 257 the progression of noble rot (gene clusters 01, 02 and 16). Expression of transcription factors 258 associated with responses to multiple stresses, such as Heat Shock Factors (HSFs; gene cluster 01 259 and 16; (Scharf et al., 2012), was also enhanced by infection.

260 Induction of hormone pathways, specifically ethylene, auxin, gibberellin and JA, was a common 261 berry response at all stages of noble rot (gene cluster 01; Figure 2). The up-regulation of 262 gibberellin biosynthetic genes was supported by the enhanced accumulation of two gibberellins at 263 stage 2 of noble rot (metabolite cluster 11). Similarly, the higher abundance of a JA and 264 derivatives at the last stages of noble rot (metabolite clusters 03 and 11) confirmed the activation 265 of JA biosynthesis and signaling pathways, including the up-regulation of JAZ proteins (gene 266 cluster 01). SA and derivatives, such as SA β-D-glucoside (e.g., a storage form of SA), 267 accumulated at most stages of noble rot (metabolite clusters 02, 03, 12, 15). A role of SA during 268 noble rot is suggested by the up-regulation at stage 1 (gene cluster 04) of a SA-responsive gene

269 with putative role in defense responses and the pathogenesis-related protein 1 precursor (*PRP1*;

- 270 VIT\_*03s0088g00710*). Hypothetically, *Botrytis* may interfere with SA signaling by expressing 271 the two fungal salicylate hydroxylases mentioned before, which could degrade the hormone 272 (Ambrose et al., 2015). Significant accumulation of abscisic acid (ABA) was detected at the last 273 stages of infection (metabolite clusters 11 and 16), while the expression of ABA biosynthetic and 274 responsive genes was differentially regulated at various stages of noble rot (Supplemental Table 275 S6).
- 276 *Botrytis* infections caused transcriptional alterations of particular primary metabolic pathways 277 (Figure 2 and Supplemental Table S8). Several processes involved in the metabolism of amino 278 acids and carbohydrates were triggered by noble rot (Figure 2). The activation of pathways 279 related to protein metabolism and modification, in particular proteolysis (gene clusters 01, 16 and

280 22; Figure 2), may explain the large number of peptides that accumulated in the noble-rotted 281 berries (metabolite clusters 02, 04, 04, 11, 12, and 15; Figure 3). The observed induction of 282 fungal proteolytic enzymes (e.g., trypsin-like proteases, cysteine proteases, serine proteases; 283 Supplemental Table S5) during noble rot may also contribute to the release of peptides. Another 284 specific feature of noble rot was the enhanced expression of fatty acid and glycerolipid 285 biosynthetic genes (gene clusters 01 and 16), which can partially explain the accumulation of 286 fatty acids, hydroxy fatty acids, glycerophospholipids and glycosphingolipids mostly at stages 2 287 and 3 of noble rot (metabolite clusters 01, 11, 12, 15). The up-regulation of unsaturated fatty acid 288 biosynthesis by *Botrytis* at stages 1 and 2 (mentioned above) could have also favored the greater 289 accumulation of lipids in the infected berries.

290 The secondary metabolism of grape berries was highly induced as noble rot progressed (Figure 2

291 and Supplemental Table S8), especially the main and peripheral phenylpropanoid pathways (gene

292 clusters 01, 16, 17 and 22). Early products of the phenylpropanoid metabolism (i.e., cinnamic

293 acid derivatives) accumulated in noble-rotted berries (metabolite clusters 01, 03, 11, 12 and 15).

294 Rosmarinic acid, a cinnamic acid derivative with antioxidant and aromatic properties (Petersen, 295 2013), showed a significant accumulation relative to control at all stages of noble rot (metabolite 296 cluster 01; Figure 3). The presence of rosmarinic acid in the noble-rotted berries was validated by proton nuclear magnetic resonance (1 297 H NMR; Supplemental Table S9). The biosynthesis of 298 stilbenes, known to be phytoalexins and strong antioxidants (Cassidy et al., 2000; Chong et al., 299 2009), was also induced in response to *Botrytis* infection (gene cluster 01). Two glycosides of a 300 resveratrol dimer (i.e., viniferin diglucosides) significantly accumulated at stage 1 (metabolite 301 clusters 03; Figure 3). The biosynthesis of flavonoids was also triggered in response to *Botrytis* 302 infection (gene clusters 01 and 16), which explained the significant accumulation of several 303 flavonoid glycosides and flavanones in noble-rotted berries (metabolite clusters 04, 11 and 15). 304 Remarkably, the biosynthesis of anthocyanins, which is normally impaired in white-skinned 305 grape berries, was activated by noble rot (gene clusters 01 and 17). Significant accumulation of cyanidin-3-rutinoside and delphinidin-3-rutinoside (confirmed by <sup>1</sup> 306 H NMR; Supplemental Table 307 S9), and cyanidin-3-gentiobioside and delphinidin-3-gentiobioside was detected at stages 1 and 2 308 of noble rot (metabolite clusters 02, 03 and 12).

309 Other aromatic compounds, including acetophenones, benzoic acid derivatives, methoxyphenols, 310 and phenolic glycosides, showed increased abundance at all stages of noble rot (metabolite cluster 311 01, 02, 03, 11, 12 and 15). These metabolites play biological roles as antioxidants and 312 antimicrobial compounds, whereas from an enological perspective they are known to modify the 313 aroma profiles of grape berries and their derived wines (Cheynier et al., 2013; Teixeira et al., 314 2013; Robinson et al., 2014). Among these, 3'-glucosyl-2',4',6'-trihydroxyacetophenone 315 accumulated at stages 1 and 2 of noble rot (Figure 3). Gallic acid, a precursor of tannin 316 biosynthesis (Yilmaz and Toledo, 2004), also had increased levels as a consequence of noble rot 317 (Figure 3, Supplemental Table S8 and Supplemental Table S9). Genes associated with the 318 biosynthesis of these phenolic derivatives showed up-regulation mostly at the initial stage of 319 noble rot (gene cluster 03; Figure 2 and Supplemental Table S8).

320 Secondary metabolic pathways involved in terpene metabolism were also up-regulated by noble 321 rot (Figure 2 and Supplemental Table S8). Besides their biological function in plant defense and 322 interaction with other organisms, terpenes can markedly influence grape and wine aroma 323 (Hjelmeland and Ebeler, 2014). Increased accumulation of mevalonic acid, a key precursor in 324 terpene biosynthesis, was detected in the noble-rotted berries (metabolite clusters 11 and 15, further validated by <sup>1</sup> 325 H NMR; Supplemental Table S9). Monoterpenes, triterpenes, sesquiterpenes, 326 and terpene glycosides accumulated at all stages of noble rot (metabolite clusters 03, 11, 12, 15).

327 While this may have resulted from the activation of plant terpene biosynthetic pathways, the 328 fungal origin of these metabolites cannot be ruled out (Collado et al., 2007).

329

## 330 **Intersection between noble rot and ripening**

331 A phenotypic hallmark of noble rot is the color change of the grape berries from yellow to pink to 332 purple-brown (Figure 1). The pink coloration that develops at the first stage of infection 333 resembles the same process that healthy berries from red-skinned varieties undergo at *véraison*. 334 The results from the analysis of responses to noble rot provided evidence that certain 335 transcriptional and metabolic changes (e.g., biosynthesis of anthocyanins) that occurred in the 336 infected white-skinned berries are similar to those that occur during normal ripening of red-337 skinned grape berries. Therefore, a meta-analysis of publicly available datasets was carried out to 338 identify alterations in developmental and metabolic pathways that are shared between *Botrytis* 339 infection and healthy ripening of grape berries. Raw RNAseq data of red-skinned grape berries at 340 two developmental stages, *pre-véraison* (i.e., touch stage) and *véraison* (i.e., soft stage), 341 belonging to five different *V. vinifera* cultivars (Sangiovese, Barbera, Negroamaro, Refosco, and 342 Primitivo) were obtained from Palumbo et al. (2014). The data from these samples were 343 processed following the same bioinformatics pipeline used for the noble-rotted samples. DESeq2 344 was used to determine DE genes between the two developmental stages of each grape variety. A 345 total of 6,847 DE genes that showed a common trend across all red-skinned varieties were 346 defined as core ripening genes (3,183 up-regulated and 3,664 down-regulated genes; 347 Supplemental Table S10).

348 Noble rot-responsive genes (i.e., DE grape genes at the noble rot stages) were compared to the 349 core ripening genes to identify changes in gene expression shared between noble rot and ripening. 350 A total of 1,382 grape genes were commonly up-regulated during noble rot and ripening, while 351 1,249 grape genes were commonly down-regulated (Supplemental Table S11). Key biological 352 processes that were concomitantly activated or suppressed as consequence of noble rot and 353 normal ripening were identified by enrichment analyses (*P* < 0.01) using the lists of common and 354 unique DE genes (Figure 4; Supplemental Table S12).

355 High expression of genes involved in the biosynthesis of amino acids, particularly aromatic 356 amino acids, was shared by *Botrytis* infection and normal development of red-skinned grape 357 berries (Figure 4). The induction of glutathione biosynthetic genes was another common response 358 between noble rot and berry ripening. Glutathione is a strong antioxidant with key roles in wine

359 aroma, particularly when it is conjugated to fatty acid derivatives (Kobayashi et al., 2011). Other

360 primary metabolic pathways showed a similar activation in noble-rotted Sémillon berries and red-

- 361 skinned berries (Figure 4). These included monosaccharide and oligosaccharide metabolism,
- 362 glycolysis and gluconeogenesis, citric acid metabolism, and glyoxylate and dicarboxylate 363 metabolism.
- 364 A prominent and common outcome of noble rot and ripening was the enhancement of the 365 phenylpropanoid metabolism (Figure 4). Figure 5 depicts the central phenylpropanoid and 366 flavonoid pathways, in which specific genes were among the up-regulated noble rot-responsive 367 genes and core ripening genes. The concurrent activation of 100 genes in the central and 368 peripheral phenylpropanoid pathways (Supplemental Table S11) suggests that similar 369 transcriptional regulators could be functioning during *Botrytis* infections of white-skinned berries 370 and *véraison* of red-skinned berries. The R2R3-MYB family of transcription factors has been

- 371 associated with the control of the phenylpropanoid metabolism during berry development (Deluc
- 372 et al., 2006; Bogs et al., 2007; Walker et al., 2007; Deluc et al., 2008; Matus et al., 2008; Höll et
- 373 al., 2013; Cavallini et al., 2015). *VvMYB5a* (*VIT\_08s0007g07230*) and *VvMYB5b*
- 374 (*VIT\_06s0004g00570*) transcription factors, which are known master regulators of the central
- 375 phenylpropanoid and flavonoid pathways in grape berries, were significantly up-regulated at
- 376 different stages of noble rot (Figure 6). The activators of stilbene biosynthesis *VvMYB14*

- 377 (*VIT\_07s0005g03340*) and *VvMYB15* (*VIT\_05s0049g01020*) are known to be induced during 378 berry ripening were highly up-regulated as noble rot progressed. The expression of a transcription 379 factor that regulates the biosynthesis of proanthocyanidins during grape berry development, 380 *VvMYBPA1* (*VIT\_15s0046g00170*), was significantly increased at stage 1 of noble rot. Two 381 suppressors of phenylpropanoid pathways, *VvMYBC2-L1* (*VIT\_01s0011g04760*) and *VvMYB4a* 382 (*VIT\_03s0038g02310*), showed differential gene expression as a consequence of noble rot. 383 Contrastingly, the transcriptional activators, *VvMYBA2* (*VIT\_02s0033g00390*) and *VvMYBF1* 384 (*VIT\_07s0005g01210*) were down-regulated at stage 2 of noble rot. *VvMYBA2* is a regulator of a 385 key anthocyanin biosynthetic gene, whereas *VvMYBF1* controls the first step of flavonol 386 biosynthesis (Czemmel et al., 2009).
- 387

### 388 **Boost in phenylpropanoid metabolism is a hallmark of noble rot**

389 The transcriptomic and metabolomic analyses described above were performed with replicated 390 grape samples harvested in 2012. To confirm that the induction of the phenylpropanoid 391 metabolism was a consistent response to noble rot and not a confound effect of a particular 392 harvest, noble-rotted and control berries collected from the same vineyard in 2013 and 2014 were 393 analyzed. Temperature and relative humidity during the growing seasons of 2012 and 2014 were 394 more suitable for the development of noble rot than during the season of 2013 (i.e., rains occurred 395 earlier and most of the *Botrytis* infections took place a month earlier than in 2012 and 2014). Due 396 to differences in the progression of noble rot, only berries at stage 3 and the asymptomatic control 397 from the harvest in 2013 were included in this study.

398 The transcriptional induction of 18 key noble-rot responsive genes in the central phenylpropanoid

399 and flavonoid pathways was confirmed by qRT-PCR (Supplemental Table S13). As shown in

- 400 figure 7, most genes presented similar patterns of expression as a result of noble rot across the
- 401 three-year evaluation; however, differences in the intensity of the up-regulations were detected.

- 402 For example, the expression of two PAL-encoding genes (*VIT\_11s0052g01090* and
- 403 *VIT\_08s0040g01710*) was higher in 2012 than in subsequent harvests. The qRT-PCR results from
- 404 samples harvested in 2012 validated the RNAseq expression data with a strong significant
- correlation (*R* = 0.95, *P* < 2.2×10-16 405 ; Figure 7).
- 406 To demonstrate that the boost in phenylpropanoid metabolism not only occurred at the
- 407 transcriptional level, the activity of key enzymes of these pathways was measured in the noble-

408 rotted and control samples collected in 2012, 2013, and 2014 as described in the Materials and 409 Methods (Figure 7). In most cases the enzyme activities reflected the patterns of gene expression. 410 A few differences were observed: (i) a constant chalcone synthases activity (CHS), and (ii) 411 increased dihydroflavonol 4-reductase activity in noble-rotted berries when compared to the 412 control.

413 To verify the activation of the anthocyanin biosynthetic pathway, total amounts of anthocyanins 414 were measured in the noble-rotted and control berries. Anthocyanin levels were significantly 415 higher in the infected samples than the control across all harvests (Figure 7). Variation in pigment 416 accumulation was expected among years because anthocyanin biosynthesis and oxidation are 417 known to be influenced by a variety of environmental factors, such as temperature, sunlight 418 exposure, and differences in the progression of noble rot (Ortega‐Regules et al., 2006; Tarara et 419 al., 2008). Specific anthocyanin classes that could explain the change in coloration of the berries 420 during noble rot include the cyanidin and delphinidin glycosides previously mentioned 421 (Supplemental Table S3 and Supplemental Table S9).

422

## 423 **Metabolic changes in noble-rotted berries reflect differences in chemical composition of** 424 **botrytized wines**

425 Botrytized wines are produced using berries at different stages of infection, ranging from stage 1 426 to stage 3 of noble rot or even later stages (i.e., *pourri roti*). Metabolic profiles of botrytized 427 wines were analyzed to determine if compound classes associated with noble rot in the vineyard 428 were detectable in the resulting wines. These corresponded to seven distinct vintages of 429 commercial botrytized wines (i.e., BOT1 wines) produced with naturally infected berries (cv. 430 Sémillon) harvested from the same vineyard blocks where the samples of this study were 431 collected. The BOT1 wines were blended with late harvest wine (e.g., Sauvignon blanc varietal, 432 non-botrytized) during their production. Additional wine samples were included in the analysis to 433 be able to distinguish specific repertoires of metabolites associated with botrytized wines: (i) five 434 botrytized wines produced by a different winery using Sémillon grape berries grown in a different 435 locations (i.e., BOT2 wines), (ii) two wines from non-botrytized Sémillon berries, and (iii) two 436 wines from non-botrytized Sauvignon blanc berries. Measurements of a specific *Botrytis* antigen 437 confirmed that the non-botrytized wines were produced from grape berries without detectable 438 *Botrytis* (see Materials and Methods for details).

439 The UHPLC/QTOF-MS approach detected a total of 299 mass features in the wines analyzed 440 (Supplemental Table S14). The abundance of the detected metabolites and the measurements of 441 the level of *Botrytis* antigen were used to construct a MFA (Figure 8). The first dimension 442 (51.76%) of the MFA separated the botrytized wines from the non-botrytized wines, while the 443 second dimension (11.66%) distinguished wine varietals (Sémillon and Sauvignon blanc). BOT1 444 and BOT2 likely landed in the middle of DIM2 since they were a blend of both varietals. 445 Supplemental Table S15 provides the details for the other dimensions calculated.

446 A correlation analysis was performed to detect metabolites that were significantly more abundant 447 (*R* ≥ 0.70, *P* ≤ 0.01) in BOT1 wines compared to non-botrytized wines. In total, 46 metabolites 448 classified in 20 compound subclasses accumulated preferentially in the wines produced from 449 noble-rotted berries (Figure 8 and Supplemental Table S16). Terpene glycosides were the most 450 abundant class in the botrytized wines and appear to be key in defining their aroma and flavor 451 profiles (Genovese et al., 2007). Products of the phenylpropanoid metabolism were expected to 452 be present in the botrytized wines as they were associated with noble rot development of grape 453 berries. Among these, 6 prenylated flavonoids, 2 flavonoid glycosides, 3 chalcone derivatives, 2 454 hydroxycinnamic acid derivatives, and one stilbene glycoside were putatively identified and had 455 enhanced levels in the botrytized wines (Supplemental Table S16). Other aromatic compounds 456 that seem to be relevant during berry responses to noble rot, such as gallic acid derivatives and 457 phenolic glycosides were also detected in the botrytized wines. Altogether these results 458 demonstrate that noble rot altered the metabolism of grape berries by activating plant stress 459 responses as well as ripening events, which altogether influenced the final chemical composition 460 of the berries and of the resulting botrytized wines.

461

462

## 463 **DISCUSSION**

## 464 **Developmental plasticity of white-skinned grape berries in response to noble rot**

- 465 In this study, we demonstrate that noble rot not only causes the activation of stress responses, but
- 466 also a reprogramming in the development of white-skinned berries, which results in the activation
- 467 of secondary metabolic pathways that are normally associated with ripening of red-skinned
- 468 cultivars. During noble rot, *Botrytis* induced the expression of transcriptional regulators that

469 function in healthy development (i.e., prior or during *véraison*) of red-skinned berries, including 470 five R2R3-MYBs that control the phenylpropanoid metabolism (Deluc et al., 2006; Bogs et al., 471 2007; Walker et al., 2007; Deluc et al., 2008; Matus et al., 2008; Höll et al., 2013). Candidate 472 master regulators of *véraison* were also up-regulated by noble rot, such as *VvNAC33* 473 (*VIT\_19s0027g00230*), *VvNAC60* (*VIT\_08s0007g07670*), a zinc-finger transcription factor 474 (*VIT\_08s0040g01950*) and a MYB transcription factor (*VIT\_07s0005g02730;* (Palumbo et al., 475 2014). Noble rot triggered the expression of 55 AP2/ERFs, most of which have been associated 476 with ripening of healthy red-skinned berries from the Corvina, Cabernet Sauvignon, and 477 Trincadeira cultivars (Deluc et al., 2007; Licausi et al., 2010; Fortes et al., 2011; Cramer et al., 478 2014). The expression of other potential berry ripening and plant senescence regulators, such as 479 the putative ortholog of *SlNAC-NOR* (*VIT\_19s0014g03300*) and all *WRKY53* genes annotated in 480 the grape genome (*VIT\_02s0025g01280*, *VIT\_15s0046g01140* and *VIT\_16s0050g02510*), was 481 also induced in noble-rotted berries. Besides their roles in berry development, some of the 482 transcription factors activated during noble rot (e.g., R2R3-MYBs and AP2-ERFs) could regulate 483 responses to biotic and abiotic stresses (Chen et al., 2012; Mizoi et al., 2012; Nakashima et al., 484 2012).

485 *Botrytis* was recently reported to induce in grape berries the expression of some transcription 486 factors associated with ripening and defense responses, mainly ERFs and WRKY33-related genes 487 (Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015). To detect berry responses that could differentiate noble rot from 488 bunch rot, we compared our transcriptomic data on noble rot of Sémillon berries with the 489 microarray data from a field-study on bunch rot of Trincadeira berries at two developmental 490 stages (Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015). This comparative analysis provides a preliminary view of 491 the transcriptional signatures of noble rot and bunch rot. Additional factors, such as discrepancies 492 in ripening stage of the berries and in the cultivars, may confound the different transcriptional 493 profiles detected in the two studies. Only 11.90% of the 12,575 noble rot responsive genes 494 showed similar expression patterns as a result of bunch rot. Among the key noble rot-responsive 495 transcription factors, differential expression of three of the R2R3-MYB activators (*VvMYB5a*, 496 *VvMYB5b*, *VvMYBPA1*) was not detected during bunch rot. Out of the 22 AP2/ERF with 497 increased gene expression during bunch rot, only 14 genes were commonly up-regulated as a 498 result of noble rot and bunch rot. Notably, *VviHRE2* (*VIT\_07s0005g00820*) and *VviRAP2.3*  499 (*VIT\_05s0077g01860*), homologs of tomato ripening regulators *SlERF2* and *SlERF6*, respectively, 500 were induced by noble rot, but not induced by bunch rot (Pirrello et al., 2006; Lee et al., 2012; 501 Cramer et al., 2014). Although, these observations suggest that distinct transcriptional regulators 502 may coordinate part of the berry responses to each type of infection, a more specific experimental

503 design (e.g., same grape cultivar, berry developmental stage and transcriptomics technology) is 504 needed to unequivocally conduct these comparisons.

505

## 506 **Metabolic plasticity in white-skinned grape berries affected with noble rot results in** 507 **distinctive wine grape flavor and aroma**

508 Noble rot induced metabolic alterations favoring the synthesis of a variety of plant compounds 509 (e.g., glycosylated and glutathionylated fatty-acid derivatives) that contribute to wine flavor and 510 aroma (Hjelmeland and Ebeler, 2014; Robinson et al., 2014). Grapes showing noble rot 511 symptoms were enriched in prenol lipids (i.e., terpenes), which were carried over into the 512 botrytized wines (Genovese et al., 2007). Besides their contribution to grape and wine aroma, 513 terpenes are involved in plant development (e.g. gibberellins) and responses to environmental 514 *stimuli* (Cheng et al., 2007). We determined that the expression of 8 terpene biosynthetic genes 515 increased during noble rot (Table I), which corresponded to the only annotated farnesyl-516 diphosphate synthase (*VvFPPS*) and distinct classes of terpene synthases (TPS; (Martin et al., 517 2010), including the functionally characterized β-caryophyllene synthase (*VvTPS13*), α-humulene 518 synthase (*VvTPS11*), and β-ocimene synthase (*VvTPS34*). When mining the microarray data from 519 bunch rot (Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015), we did not detect increase of expression of *VvFPPS* or 520 any of the sixty-nine candidate grape TPSs (Martin et al., 2010). Notably, these genes were either 521 not differentially expressed or down-regulated in the bunch-rotted berries (Agudelo-Romero et al., 522 2015). Moreover, the levels of grape terpenes have been reported to decrease during typical 523 *Botrytis* infections (Shimizu et al., 1982; Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015), as *Botrytis* is capable to 524 degrade and transform plant-derived terpenes (Collado et al., 2007). These observations indicate 525 that the accumulation of terpenes may be a specific feature of noble rot.

526 A number of important wine flavor volatiles are produced from the lipoxygenase pathway during 527 berry development (Schwab et al., 2008). Lipoxygenases (LOXs) catalyze the dioxygenation of 528 unsaturated fatty acids as one of the initial steps in the synthesis of wide range of oxylipins 529 (Liavonchanka and Feussner, 2006). As a consequence of noble rot, *VvLOXB*, *VvLOXC*, *VvLOXO*, 530 *VvLOXK*, and *VvLOXP* were up-regulated (Table I). Jasmonates are C12 oxylipins with important 531 roles in hormone signaling and defense responses against fungal pathogens (Wasternack, 2007). 532 Therefore, the induction of several lipoxygenases and the activation of the jasmonate signaling 533 network appear to be a common outcome of *Botrytis* infections in grape berries, and are not 534 specific to noble rot or berry ripening (Podolyan et al., 2010; Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015).

535 C6 oxylipins (e.g., C6 aldehydes, alcohols and esters) are important determinants of grape berry 536 aroma (Baumes, 2009), and are considered to be the precursors of volatile thiols that define key 537 aromas in several white wine varietals, Bordeaux reds and botrytized wines (Bouchilloux et al., 538 1998; Sarrazin et al., 2007; Bailly et al., 2009; Lund et al., 2009). High contents of 3- 539 sulfanylhexanol (3SH), 3-sulfanylpentan-1-ol (3SP) and 3-sulfanylheptan-1-ol (3SHp) provide 540 most of the fruit and citrus aromas in botrytized wines (Sarrazin et al., 2007; Thibon et al., 2009). 541 The proposed pathway for the generation of volatile thiols starts with the conjugation of a C6 542 oxylypin, such as (E)-2-hexenal, to glutathione by GSTs (Kobayashi et al., 2011). Glutathione 543 conjugations are part of the detoxification pathways that occur during normal grape berry 544 development and as a response to multiple stresses (Dixon et al., 2010; Zamboni et al., 2010). We 545 detected up-regulation of 50 GSTs during all the stages of noble rot, including two specific GSTs 546 (*VvGST3* and *VvGST4*) that have been demonstrated to be involved in the generation of volatile 547 thiols (Table I; (Kobayashi et al., 2011). Only 7 GST-encoding genes were significantly up-548 regulated due to bunch rot (Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015). The biosynthesis of volatile thiols 549 proceeds with the breakdown of the glutathione S-conjugates by γ–glutamyltranspeptidases 550 (GGTs) and carboxypeptidases to generate cysteine S–conjugates. We determined that a putative 551 grape GGT gene and two candidate carboxypeptidases were among the up-regulated noble rot-552 responsive genes, and none of these had differential expression during bunch rot (Table I; 553 (Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015). The cysteine S–conjugates are then cleaved during alcoholic 554 fermentation to produce a wide variety of volatile thiols (Thibon et al., 2010). A 3SP cysteine S– 555 conjugate drastically accumulated in berries at stages 2 and 3 of noble rot, while being absent in 556 healthy berries, which provides further evidence that the biosynthesis of volatile thiol precursors 557 is highly induced during noble rot of Sémillon berries.

558

## 559 **Noble rot induction of phenylpropanoid metabolism: intersection between plant stress** 560 **responses and ripening-associated events**

561 Noble rot stimulated the production of phenolic compounds that derive from the shikimate and 562 phenylpropanoid metabolism. This could explain the higher levels of polyphenols that are present 563 in botrytized wines when compared to non-botrytized white wines (Nikfardjam et al., 2006). 564 Phenolic compounds play important roles in the flavor and aroma, color, physical mouth-feel and 565 health-promoting properties of wine (Gawel, 1998; Boulton, 2001; Waterhouse, 2002). In the 566 context of plant-pathogen interactions, they have antimicrobial functions and protect the berry 567 tissues from oxidation (Cheynier et al., 2013; Teixeira et al., 2013). We confirmed the induction 568 of the shikimate metabolism by the enhanced expression of enzymes involved in the flux towards 569 gallic acid biosynthesis (e.g., a shikimate dehydrogenase; Table I) and by the accumulation of 570 gallic acid and derivatives in both noble-rotted berries and botrytized wines.

571 A strong induction of the initial steps in the phenylpropanoid metabolism was found in noble-572 rotted berries. Phenylalanine ammonia-lyases (PALs) catalyze the non-oxidative deamination of 573 phenylalanine to *trans*-cinnamic acid. PAL gene expression and activity have been reported to be 574 absent or reduced in white-skinned berries when compared to red-skinned berries (Boss et al., 575 1996). During noble rot, we observed the up-regulation of 15 PAL-encoding genes, most of 576 which are also induced during *véraison* and bunch rot of red-skinned berries (Palumbo et al., 577 2014; Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015). The expression of two of these genes, *VvPAL1* and *VvPAL2*, 578 has been reported to increase during ripening of the white-skinned Chardonnay berries (Table I; 579 (Guillaumie et al., 2011); however their up-regulation is not as pronounced as in noble-rotted 580 grape berries: 1-fold change (log2) in Chardonnay berries compared to more than 3-fold change 581 (log2) in *Botrytis*-infected Sémillon berries. *Trans*-cinnamate 4-monooxygenases (C4Hs) and 4- 582 coumaroyl-CoA ligases (4CLs) are indispensable for all subsequent steps of the central and 583 peripheral phenylpropanoid pathways (Weisshaar and Jenkins, 1998; Winkel-Shirley, 1999). The 584 up-regulation of three C4H-encoding genes and 8 4CL-encoding genes was validated by their 585 enhanced enzymatic activities in noble-rotted berries collected during the 2012, 2013 and 2014 586 harvests (Table I). The expression of these biosynthetic genes also increased during bunch rot and 587 *véraison* of red-skinned cultivars (Palumbo et al., 2014; Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015). 588 Activation of the phenylpropanoid metabolism is known to occur in response to oxidative stress 589 in plant tissues (Grace and Logan, 2000; Vranová et al., 2002; Cheynier et al., 2013). Therefore, 590 accumulation of reactive oxygen species (ROS) at *véraison* or as consequence of *Botrytis* 591 infections may explain the common induction of the central phenylpropanoid pathway (Torres et 592 al., 2006; Pilati et al., 2014).

593 Our data showed that the activation of the pathways leading to lignin and stilbene biosynthesis is 594 a common response of grape berries to noble rot and bunch rot (Jeandet et al., 1995; Agudelo-595 Romero et al., 2015). Several genes encoding putative lignin biosynthetic enzymes were up-596 regulated in noble-rotted berries, including 5 caffeic acid 3-*O*-methyltransferases, 7 cinnamyl 597 alcohol dehydrogenases, 6 cinnamoyl-CoA reductases and 23 laccases. The up-regulation of 40 598 genes coding for stilbene synthases (STSs) together with the accumulation of stilbene glycosides 599 in infected berries and botrytized wine verified the induction of stilbene biosynthesis during noble 600 rot. Some stilbene dimers, in particular ε-viniferin glycosides, have been reported to be more 601 effective phytoalexins and to have stronger health-promoting effects than the monomeric 602 stilbenoids (Schouten et al., 2002; Zghonda et al., 2012). The concomitant expression of the STSs 603 directly correlated with the highly significant up-regulation of the *VvMYB14* and *VvMYB15*  604 transcription factors, which are the master regulators of the stilbene pathway (Höll et al., 2013). 605 Although stilbenes are generally induced in berries under biotic and abiotic stress, they also 606 accumulate after *véraison* in healthy red-skinned and white-skinned berries (Gatto et al., 2008; 607 Höll et al., 2013).

608 Key steps in flavonoid biosynthesis were triggered by noble rot. Consistently over the three years 609 of sampling, a chalcone isomerase (CHI), five flavanone 3-hydroxylases (F3Hs) and a DFR were 610 induced at the transcriptional level and displayed higher enzymatic activities in noble-rotted 611 berries (Table I). During bunch rot, CHI-encoding genes did not show differential expression, 612 whereas DFR-encoding genes were either not differentially regulated or down-regulated 613 (Agudelo-Romero et al., 2015). The induction of the main flavonoid biosynthetic genes in noble 614 rotted-berries can be explained by the significant up-regulation of the transcriptional activators 615 *VvMYB5a* and *VvMYB5b*, and the down-regulation of the transcriptional inhibitor *VvMYB2-L1*, 616 all of which have been shown to control this pathway (Deluc et al., 2006; Deluc et al., 2008; 617 Cavallini et al., 2015). On the other hand, the production of flavones and flavonols is suppressed 618 during noble rot. The reduced levels of these flavonoids in noble-rotted berries were congruent 619 with the significant down-regulation of the transcriptional regulator *VvMYBF1* (Figure 6; 620 (Czemmel et al., 2009), and the flavonol synthase (FLS) gene expression and activity.

621 Interestingly, noble rot induced the synthesis and accumulation of anthocyanins in Sémillon 622 berries (Figure 5 and Figure 7). To our knowledge, this is the first report of anthocyanin 623 accumulation in berries from a white-skinned grape cultivar as result of pathogen infection. 624 White-skinned berries arose from multi-allelic mutations in the *VvMYBA1* and *VvMYBA2* genes, 625 which regulate the gene expression of a UDP-glucose:flavonoid 3-*O*-glucosyltransferase 626 (*VvUF3GT*; (Kobayashi et al., 2004; Lijavetzky et al., 2006; This et al., 2007; Walker et al., 2007; 627 Shimazaki et al., 2011). UF3GTs catalyze the 3-*O*-glycosylation of anthocyanidins (Kobayashi et 628 al., 2004). The expression of *VvUF3GT* gene and the resultant enzymatic activity are absent in 629 white-skinned berries during normal development (Boss et al., 1996; Kobayashi et al., 2001). 630 Although the grapevine genome contains at least 22 putative UF3GTs (Grimplet et al., 2009), 631 only *VvUF3GT* (*VIT\_16s0039g02230*) has been widely studied. Even though this *UF3GT* was 632 not significantly up-regulated, the expression of 6 putative *UF3GTs* increased in response to 633 noble rot (Table I) and during ripening in red-skinned berries (Ali et al., 2011; Palumbo et al., 634 2014). The up-regulation of these genes resulted in enhanced UF3GT activity in the noble-rotted 635 berries across all three growing seasons. We did not detect the expression of *VvMYBA1* in the 636 noble-rotted berries and only found a significant down-regulation of *VvMYBA2* during late stages 637 of noble rot. The expression of *VvMYBA2* is not expected to influence anthocyanin biosynthesis 638 in Sémillon berries because this cultivar carries a mutated allele of this gene, which encodes a 639 non-functional protein (Walker et al., 2007). Therefore, this information suggests that 640 transcriptional regulators independent or downstream of *VvMYBA1/2* control the expression of 641 the additional UF3GT genes and, that signaling molecule derived from the *Botrytis*-grape berry 642 interaction may activate this pathway.

643 The up-regulation of 35 genes with potential functions in the regulation of anthocyanin content 644 and composition provided further evidence of the activation of anthocyanin metabolism in the 645 noble-rotted berries (Costantini et al., 2015). These included genes encoding several transcription 646 regulators (e.g., *VIT\_02s0033g00450*, *VIT\_09s0018g00240*, *VIT\_10s0116g01200*, 647 *VIT\_12s0028g03270*), hormone responsive proteins (e.g., *VIT\_03s0180g00320*, 648 *VIT\_04s0023g03600*, *VIT\_12s0057g00420*, *VIT\_17s0000g07560*), and enzymes involved in 649 anthocyanin modifications (e.g., *VIT\_18s0041g00710*, *VIT\_18s0041g00740*, 650 *VIT\_18s0041g00840*). Two glutathione S-transferases (*VvGST1* and *VvGST4*), shown to transport 651 anthocyanins from the cytosol to the vacuole (Conn et al., 2008), also had higher expression in 652 the noble-rotted berries (Table I). These observations confirmed that the induction of anthocyanin 653 metabolic pathways is a hallmark of noble rot in Sémillon berries.

654

## 655 *Botrytis* **infection strategies during noble rot**

656 Future research focused on the understanding of the virulence factors and the signaling molecules 657 produced by *Botrytis* to promote susceptibility in grape berries still needs to be pursued. These 658 signals may include plant hormones, small RNAs, ROS and/or pathogen- and damage-associated 659 molecular patterns generated during the *Botrytis* infection (Melotto et al., 1994; Chagué et al., 660 2002; Siewers et al., 2004; Sharon et al., 2007; Segmüller et al., 2008; Weiberg et al., 2013).

661 We report here that several plant hormone networks were altered during noble rot of white-662 skinned berries, including ethylene, auxin, JA, gibberellin, SA, and ABA pathways. ABA and 663 ethylene are known modulators of both berry ripening and plant responses to pathogen attack 664 (Chervin et al., 2004; van Loon et al., 2006; Ton et al., 2009; Wheeler et al., 2009; Böttcher et al., 665 2013; Nicolas et al., 2014). *Botrytis* is capable to produce ethylene via the keto-γ-666 methyltiobutyric acid pathway (Chagué et al., 2002) and ABA from farnesyl diphosphate 667 (Siewers et al., 2006). However, we did not find strong transcriptional evidence that the pathogen 668 was producing these two hormones (i.e., no significant up-regulation of biosynthetic genes) 669 during noble rot of Sémillon berries. Instead, the data suggested that *Botrytis* infection caused an 670 imbalance in the berry synthesis and perception of hormones, which in turn activated several 671 ripening-associated pathways and facilitated the colonization of the host tissues. The defense 672 mechanisms established by the plant, although could have slowed down the infection, were 673 inadequate to contain the spread of *Botrytis* mycelia in the berry. *Botrytis* may also rely on 674 virulence factors, such as fungal protein phosphatases (Amselem et al., 2011; Giesbert et al., 675 2012; Yang et al., 2013), to alter plant-signaling cascades (i.e., MAPK cascades) that are crucial 676 for berry development and responses to environmental cues. An experimental setup that allows a 677 direct comparison between noble rot and bunch rot will be necessary to define and further 678 characterize the specific molecular mechanisms employed by *Botrytis*.

679 Overall our study shows that the developmental and metabolic plasticity displayed by white-680 skinned grape berries during noble rot result from two opposing effects: (i) the induction of 681 ripening-associated pathways by *Botrytis* to promote susceptibility and (ii) the activation of plant 682 responses to cope against multiple stresses that result from the interaction with the pathogen.

683

#### 684 **MATERIALS AND METHODS**

#### 685 **Biological material**

686 Ripe grape (*Vitis vinifera* cv. Sémillon) berries with symptoms of noble rot, caused by *Botrytis*  687 *cinerea* strain BcDW1 (Blanco-Ulate et al., 2013b) and other *Botrytis* strains present in the field, 688 were harvested from the Dolce Winery vineyard (Oakville, California, USA) on November 6 689 2012, October 13 2013 and November 6 2014. These dates were chosen to coincide with the days 690 of harvest selected by the Dolce winemaker for the production of botrytized wines. All sample 691 collections were performed between 8:00am and 10:00am. The weather conditions at the time of 692 collection were: 17.2 ± 3.4 ºC with a relative humidity of 68.3 ± 12.1% for the harvest of 2012, 693 10.6 ± 5.1 ºC with a relative humidity of 83.0 ± 14.7% for the harvest of 2013, and 14.4 ± 3.1 ºC 694 with a relative humidity of 78.0 ± 15.0% for the harvest of 2014.

695 Three distinct stages of noble rot (stages 1-3) were determined based on visual assessment of the 696 severity of the symptoms and confirmed by measurements of fungal biomass (described below). 697 At the same time, ripe berries without visible *Botrytis* infection were harvested as asymptomatic 698 controls. During the three independent harvests, samples were randomly collected from vines in 699 the same rows of the vineyard (Supplemental Figure S2). Individual berries were collected from 700 clusters at different positions in the vine to avoid confounding effects due to differences in cluster 701 sun exposure and others. Five biological replicates consisting of independent pools of 20 berries 702 from at least five different vines were obtained per each stage of infection and asymptomatic 703 control. Berries of each biological replicate were deseeded, frozen, and ground (skin and pulp) to 704 powder in liquid nitrogen. Soluble solid measurements on the homogenized biological replicates 705 were performed.

706 Commercial control and botrytized wines were used for metabolomic analyses. These correspond 707 to seven vintages (2005, 2007, 2008, 2009, 2011, 2012 and 2013) of botrytized wines produced 708 with berries from the Dolce Winery vineyard, five vintages (1985, 1997, 2000, 2001 and 2010) of 709 botrytized wines produced with Sémillon berries by another winery in Napa Valley (California, 710 USA), two vintages (2010 and 2013 produced in Australia) of non-botrytized Sémillon wine, and 711 two vintages (2012 produced in California and 2013 produced in New Zealand) of non-botrytized 712 Sauvignon blanc wine.

### 713 *Botrytis cinerea* **biomass measurements**

714 The amount of fungal biomass present in the noble-rotted grape berries and botrytized wines was 715 measured with the QuickStix Kit for *Botrytis cinerea* (EnviroLogix Inc.), which utilizes the 716 monoclonal antibody BC12.CA4 (Meyer and Dewey, 2000). Fungal biomass quantification in 717 grape berries was performed with 1 g of ground tissue from each biological replicate suspended 718 in the kit buffer, 1:5 m/v for the control samples, 1:20 m/v for the samples at stage 1, 1:250 m/v 719 for the samples at stage 2, and 1:500 m/v for samples at stage 3. The presence of the BC12.CA4 720 antigen in the wines was measured using dilutions of 1:10 v/v for the control wines and 1:800 v/v 721 for the botrytized wines. The antibody cross-reactive material was quantified in 500 μL of the 722 tissue suspension or wine dilution, and the intensity of the test line was determined using the 723 QuickStix reader (EnviroLogix Inc.). The material that cross-reacted with the antibody was 724 measured in 500 μL of the tissue suspension or wine dilution. Two standard curves were obtained 725 using known amounts of dry *Botrytis* mycelia spiked into tissue extracts from control berries (y = 2.36x – 7.10; *R<sup>2</sup>* =0.96) and into control wines (y = 2.09x - 28.39, *R<sup>2</sup>* 726 =0.98). The signal intensity 727 measured by the QuickStix reader was then converted into fungal biomass (μg/g FW of berry or 728 μg/mL of wine) based on the standard curves.

## 729 **Analyses of sugars and sugar alcohols**

730 Samples were prepared by diluting 1 g of the ground tissue per biological replicate into 2 mL of 731 deionized water (1:2 m/v). Sugars and sugar alcohols were analyzed in an Agilent 1100 HPLC 732 System coupled to a Hewlett-Packard 1047A Refractive Index Detector (Agilent Technologies, 733 Inc.). Three technical replications of each sample were loaded into a Bio-Rad Aminex HPX-87C 734 calcium form carbohydrate analysis column (300 mm x 7.8 mm, Bio-Rad Laboratories, Inc., 735 USA) and run using the following method: water as mobile phase, 0.6 mL/min (isocratic) flow 736 rate, 80 °C column temperature, 20 µL sample injection volume, 25 minutes run time. Standards 737 for glucose, fructose, glycerol, mannitol and sorbitol (Sigma-Aldrich Corp.) were run in parallel 738 with the samples and used to obtain standard curves. Data acquisition and analyses were 739 performed with the Agilent ChemStation for LC 3D, Rev. A.10.02 software (Agilent 740 Technologies, Inc., USA).

#### 741 **RNA extraction**

742 Five biological replicates representing the three stages of noble rot and the control from the three 743 harvesting seasons (2012, 2013 and 2014) were used for RNA extraction. Total RNA was 744 obtained from 2 g of ground tissue (skin and pulp) using the protocol described in Blanco-Ulate et 745 al. (2013a). The concentration and purity of the RNA were measured using the NanoDrop 2000c 746 Spectrophotometer (Thermo Scientific, Inc., USA). RNA integrity was confirmed by agarose gel 747 electrophoresis.

### 748 **mRNA sequencing and bioinformatics pipeline**

749 Sequencing of mRNA was performed with four biological replicates of each noble rot stage and 750 control from the 2012 harvest. cDNA libraries were prepared from 4 μg of total RNA using the 751 Illumina TruSeq RNA Sample preparation kit v.2 with the low-throughput protocol (Illumina, 752 Inc., USA). Each cDNA library was barcoded individually and analyzed for quantity and quality 753 with the High Sensitivity DNA Analysis Kit in the Agilent 2100 Bioanalyzer (Agilent 754 Technologies, Inc.). cDNA libraries were combined in equal amounts for sequencing (single end, 755 50 bp) at the Expression Analysis Core Facility (University of California, Davis) in an Illumina 756 HiSeq 2000 sequencer.

757 Quality trimming and filtering of the Illumina reads was done with Sickle v.1.21 758 (https://github.com/ucdavis-bioinformatics/sickle) with a threshold of 20 (Q > 20) and Scythe 759 v.0.991 (https://github.com/ucdavis-bioinformatics/scythe) with a prior of 0.4. A non-redundant *B.*  760 *cinerea* mapping reference was obtained by combining B05.10 transcripts 761 (http://www.broadinstitute.org/annotation/genome/botrytis\_cinerea) with BcDW1

762 (http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/AORW00000000.1) transcript using CD-HIT (v. 4.5.4). 763 *Botrytis* transcripts were combined with the predicted transcriptome of *Vitis vinifera* cv. PN40024 764 (http://www.genoscope.cns.fr/externe/Download/Projets/Projet\_ML/data/12X/annotation/) and 765 used as a reference for mapping the quality-trimmed reads with Bowtie2 v.2.1.0 (Langmead and 766 Salzberg, 2012). The mapping parameters were as follow: -q --end-to-end --sensitive --no-unal - 767 p20. The resultant SAM files were parsed to obtain read counts per grape gene with the python 768 script sam2counts.py v.0.91 (https://github.com/vsbuffalo/sam2counts). The Bioconductor 769 package DESeq2 (Love et al., 2014) was used to normalize the read counts of *Botrytis* and grape 770 genes across the biological replicates of *Botrytis*-infected and control samples. Differential 771 expression analyses of the grape genes across stages of noble rot were tested with DESeq2.

772 The DESeq2-normalized counts of *Botrytis* and grape transcripts for each of the samples, their 773 correspondent mass feature abundances (see below) and biomass measurements (see above) were 774 used as quantitative variables for a multiple factor analysis (MFA) performed in R with the 775 FactoMineR package (http://factominer.free.fr). Only quantitative variables that were different 776 than zero in at least one of the samples were used for the MFA, which included: (i) 24,373 grape 777 genes, (ii) 15,550 *Botrytis* genes, and (iii) 2,084 mass features.

778 The functional annotation of *Botrytis* genes was performed using BLASTP (NCBI nr Asomycota database; e-value < 10−3 779 , -v 10 -b 10, -F F) followed by gene ontology (GO) annotation using 780 Blast2GO (Conesa et al., 2005). GO enrichments among the *Botrytis* genes were performed using 781 the Bioconductor package topGO 782 (http://www.bioconductor.org/packages/release/bioc/html/topGO.html) and enriched GO terms (*P*  783 ≤ 0.05) were visualized using the 'Reduce + Visualize Gene Ontology' (REViGO) webserver 784 (http://revigo.irb.hr; (Supek et al., 2011). Grape genes were classified into functional categories 785 and subcategories using the annotations provided in VitisNet 786 (https://www.sdstate.edu/ps/research/vitis/pathways.cfm; (Grimplet et al., 2009). Enrichment 787 analyses of grape biological functions were computed in R using a hypergeometric test and a cut-788 off of *P* ≤ 0.01 was set to determine statistical significance.

789 For the comparative analysis between the responses of Sémillon berries to noble rot and the 790 processes that occur during ripening of red-skinned berries, raw Illumina reads from red-skinned 791 grape berries (cultivars Sangiovese, Barbera, Negroamaro, Refosco, and Primitivo) at three 792 developmental stages were downloaded from GEO (GSE62744). Data were processed, mapped, 793 normalized and analyzed for differential expression following the same methods described above.

### 794 **UHPLC-MS/MS and classification of metabolites into compound classes**

33 Downloaded from [www.plantphysiol.org](http://www.plantphysiol.org/) on October 13, 2015 - Published by [www.plant.org](http://www.plant.org) Copyright © 2015 American Society of Plant Biologists. All rights reserved.

795 Five biological replicates of noble-rotted and control samples from the harvest of 2012 were used 796 for metabolite profiling. The samples were prepared by diluting 1 gram of the ground tissue per 797 biological replicate into 2 mL of deionized water (1:2 m/v). Two independent dilutions were 798 performed for each biological replication. Three technical replications (i.e., machine replications) 799 of each dilution were analyzed using the Agilent 1290 ultra-high pressure liquid chromatograph 800 (UHPLC) coupled to an Agilent 6530 Quadrupole Time-of-Flight (QTOF) mass spectrometer 801 (Agilent Technologies Inc.) as described in Collins et al. (2014).

802 Initial processing of the metabolomics data was performed with the Agilent MassHunter 803 Qualitative Analysis software v.6.00 (Agilent Technologies, Inc.). The Molecular Feature 804 Extractor (MFE) tool was used to determine the presence of non-redundant mass features by 805 screening potential peaks for the presence of a C13 isotope, and to detect any adduct ions that 806 might be present. MFE was run using default settings with the peak filter set to include only 807 peaks larger than 1,000 counts or approximately three-times the electronic noise. Mass features 808 found using MFE were further processed with the Agilent Mass Profiler Professional (MPP) 809 software v.12.1. The default retention time and mass windows settings of 0.1 min and 5.0 ppm, 810 respectively, were used to align the mass features across the dataset with a minimum absolute 811 abundance of 5,000 counts. Mean retention times and mass sizes of each feature are reported in 812 Supplemental Table S3 and Supplemental Table S14. Feature signal intensities were scaled using 813 Z-transformation as implemented in the MPP software.

814 After data processing, the resulting abundance of each mass feature was averaged to obtain a 815 value for each of the two independent dilutions performed per sample. If a mass feature was 816 detected in only one out of the three machine replications, the abundance was set to zero as it is 817 likely to represent a software miscall or noise. The mass feature abundance of both independent 818 dilutions was then averaged to obtain a value per biological replication. If the mass feature 819 abundance in one of the dilutions was zero (i.e., no detection or detection in only one machine 820 replication), we reported the abundance measured in the other dilution. Differential accumulation 821 of metabolites at the noble rot stages S1, S2, and S3 was determined by *t*-tests against the non-822 symptomatic control samples (C). *P-*values were corrected using the Benjamini-Hochberg 823 method implemented in the R "stats" package. A cut-off of *Padj* ≤ 0.05 was applied.

824 Possible compound identities were obtained based on mass matches to the METLIN metabolite 825 database (https://metlin.scripps.edu; (Smith et al., 2005) using a 30 ppm mass window. As MFE 826 provides the neutral mass [M], the database was only searched for the neutral charge. Based on 827 statistical significance (i.e., in the MFA and the *t*-tests), a set of 688 metabolites (642 compounds 828 from control and noble-rotted berries, and 46 from wines) was manually curated by evaluating 829 their retention time and MS/MS spectra. Polarity of the potential matches was compared to the 830 polarity of authentic standards of a range of polarities whose retention times in this 831 chromatographic system had been previously established. MS/MS fragmentation data were 832 collected in a data-dependent automated mode after UHPLC. The data obtained was qualitatively 833 compared to available spectra in METLIN and the Tandem Mass Spectrum Database 834 (http://www.tmsdatabase.org). The manually-curated metabolites were then assigned to classes 835 and subclasses based on the chemical taxonomy established in the Human Metabolome Database 836 v.3.0 (HMDB; http://www.hmdb.ca; (Wishart et al., 2012).

837

## 838 **NMR analyses**

839 Three biological replicates of noble-rotted and control samples from the harvests of 2012, 2013 840 and 2014 were used to confirm the identities of 7 metabolites using an NMR approach 841 (Supplemental Table S9). Metabolites were chosen for NMR analysis based on three criteria: (i) 842 they had significant accumulation in (*Padj* ≤ 0.05) in most of the noble rot stages, (ii) they 843 represented compounds with biological significance (e.g. products of the phenylpropanoid 844 pathway and plant hormones), and (iii) most presented an unambiguous compound identity based 845 on mass, retention time and MS/MS spectra. The chemical extraction was done in 50 mg of 846 freeze-dried grape tissues using methanol:chloroform:water (2:2:1) following by a sonication for 847 1 h. After centrifugation at 11,000 rpm for 20 min at 4 ºC, the upper hydro-alcoholic phase was 848 carefully separated and dried under speed-vac for 24 h. To evaluate the reproducibility of sample 849 preparation, three portions of one of the samples were used to perform three extractions. For the <sup>1</sup> 850 H-NMR quantification, the dry residue of the hydro-alcoholic phase was dissolved in 0.75 mL of 851 D2O phosphate buffer (150 mM, pD 7.0) containing 3 mM of 4,4-trimethyl-4-silyl propanoic acid 852 (TSP) as internal standard.

All <sup>1</sup> 853 H NMR spectra were recorded at 298 K on a Bruker AVIII HD 500 NMR spectrometer (500.13 MHz for <sup>1</sup> 854 H) equipped with a 5 mm CPP BBO cryogenic probe (Bruker Biospin, Germany). <sup>1</sup> H spectra were referenced to TSP signal (δ = 0.00 ppm), whereas <sup>13</sup> 855 C spectra were 856 referenced to CH-1 resonance of α-D-glucose (δ = 93.10 ppm). A standard one-dimensional pulse 857 sequence noesypr1d (recycle delay-90°-t1-90°-tm -90°-acquisition) was used to obtain metabolic 858 profiles of the grape berry extracts with the 90° pulse length of about 11 µs and t1 of 3 µs. Water 859 suppression was achieved with a weak irradiation during the recycle delay (RD = 2 s) and mixing 860 time (t m = 100 ms). 64 transients were collected into 32,768 data points for each spectrum with a spectral width of 12 kHz. An exponential window function with line-broadening factor of 0.5 Hz
was applied to free induction decays (FIDs) prior to Fourier transformation (FT).

For resonance assignment purposes, <sup>1</sup>H -<sup>1</sup>H COSY (cosygpqfpr), <sup>1</sup>H -<sup>1</sup>H TOCSY (mlevphpr) and 863 <sup>1</sup>H - <sup>13</sup>C HSQC (hsqcetgp) 2D NMR spectra were acquired from the HMDB (Wishart et al., 2012) 864 and from previous literature (Nakayama et al., 1999; Seeram et al., 2002; Du et al., 2008; Abedini 865 866 et al., 2013), in which complex mixtures were used to specifically and selectively identify the 867 chemical shifts. In COSY and TOCSY experiments, 48 transients were collected into 2,048 data 868 points for each of 256 increments with the spectral width of 10 ppm for both dimensions. Phase 869 insensitive mode was used with gradient selection for the COSY experiments whereas the well-870 known MLEV-17 was employed as the spin-lock scheme in the phase sensitive. The TOCSY experiment (TPPI) had a mixing time of 100 ms. <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>13</sup>C HSQC NMR spectra were determined 871 872 using the gradient selected sequences with 200 transients and 2.048 data points for each of 128 increments. The spectral widths were 26,410 Hz for <sup>1</sup>H and 26 410 Hz for <sup>13</sup>C in HSQC 873 874 experiments. The data were Fourier transformed into a 4 x 2k matrix with appropriate apodization functions. <sup>1</sup>H NMR spectra were manually corrected for phase and baseline distortions using 875 876 TOPSPIN (V3.2, Bruker Biospin) and the spectral region of  $\delta$  0.5-9.5 was uniformly integrated 877 into 3,166 buckets with width of 0.003 ppm (1.8 Hz) using the Chenomx software (v3.8.3, Bruker 878 Biospin). The region  $\delta = 4.67-5.15$  was discarded to eliminate the effects of imperfect water pre-879 saturation. The spectral areas of all buckets were normalized to the weight of extracts employed 880 for measurements. The intensities of 30 selected <sup>1</sup>H resonances due hydro-alcoholic metabolites 881 were measured with respect to the intensity of TSP signal used as an internal standard with a 882 concentration of 3 mM.

## 883 Quantitative reverse transcription PCR (qRT-PCR)

884 cDNA was prepared from total RNA using M-MLV Reverse Transcriptase (Promega). qRT-PCR 885 was performed on a StepOnePlus PCR System using Fast SYBR Green Master Mix (Applied 886 Biosystems). The qRT-PCR conditions used to amplify all genes were: 95 °C for 10 min, 887 followed by 40 cycles of 95 °C for 3 s and 60 °C for 30 s. Based on their previously reported stability in grape berries (Licausi et al., 2010), VvActin (VIT 04s0044g00580) and VvUbiquitin 888 (VIT 16s0098g01190) were initially chosen as reference genes to linearize the transcript levels 889 for all genes of interest using the formula 2<sup>(Reference gene CT- Gene of interest CT)</sup> (Chen and Dubcovsky. 890 891 2012). The linearized values correspond to the relative gene expression within a given sample and 892 are comparable across genes. Three to five biological replicates of the *Botrytis*-infected and 893 control grape berries were used to obtain the relative gene expression data. Supplemental Table

894 S12 provides the sequences of the primers designed for this study.
